institution and a failing institution may possess similar
budgets. A living civilization and a dying civilization
may possess comparable resources.

The difference lies not in their current state but in
their future possibility structure.

Reachability theory provides a mathematical framework for
describing this distinction.

Throughout Chapters~13--31 reachability volume serves as
one of the principal quantities used to characterize
repair, regeneration, intelligence, governance,
admissibility, and future preservation.

\subsection{Control Systems}

Let

\[
\dot x
=

f(x,u)
\]

be a controlled dynamical system.

Here

\[
x(t)\in X
\]

denotes the state of the system and

\[
u(t)\in U
\]

denotes a control input.

The function

\[
f
\]

determines the local dynamics.

Unlike autonomous systems, controlled systems possess
multiple possible futures.

The control variable selects among them.

\begin{definition}{Admissible Control}{adm-control}
A control function

\[
u(t)
\]

is admissible if it satisfies all imposed constraints.
\end{definition}

Examples include physical force limitations, resource
constraints, informational constraints, ecological
constraints, and admissibility constraints.

\subsection{Reachable Sets}

\begin{definition}{Reachable Set}{reachable-set}
Let

\[
x_0
\]

be an initial state.

The reachable set at time (t) is

\[
R_t(x_0)
=

\left{
x(t)
:
u(\tau)
\text{ admissible}
\right}.
\]
\end{definition}

The reachable set contains every state that can be
attained from the initial condition within time (t).

Reachability therefore converts dynamics into geometry.

Instead of asking what trajectory occurs, we ask what
region of possibility remains accessible.

\begin{ex}
For

\[
\dot x=u,
\qquad
|u|\le1,
\]

the reachable set is

\[
R_t
=

[-t,t].
\]

Reachability grows linearly with time.
\end{ex}

\subsection{Reachability Volume}

The size of a reachable region is measured by its volume.

\begin{definition}{Reachability Volume}{reachability-volume}
Let

\[
\mu
\]

be a measure on the state space.

The reachability volume is

\[
\Vol_R(t)
=

\mu(R_t).
\]
\end{definition}

Reachability volume measures future flexibility.

Large values indicate many accessible futures.

Small values indicate restricted possibility.

\begin{rem}
Throughout this text reachability volume plays a role
analogous to phase-space volume in statistical mechanics.
\end{rem}

\subsection{Monotonicity of Reachability}

Reachable sets frequently expand through time.

\begin{theorem}{Reachability Monotonicity}{reachability-monotonicity}
Suppose controls remain available throughout the interval

\[
[0,t_2].
\]

Then

\[
R_{t_1}
\subseteq
R_{t_2}
\]

for

\[
t_1<t_2.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Any trajectory achievable in time

\[
t_1
\]

can be followed by a period of zero control until

\[
t_2.
\]

Hence every state reachable at

\[
t_1
\]

remains reachable at

\[
t_2.
\]

Therefore

\[
R_{t_1}
\subseteq
R_{t_2}.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
Reachability volume is nondecreasing:

\[
\Vol_R(t_1)
\le
\Vol_R(t_2).
\]
\end{corollary}

This result describes idealized systems.

Real systems often experience reachability loss due to
resource depletion, damage, lock-in effects, or
admissibility collapse.

\subsection{Reachability Entropy}

The raw volume of a reachable set depends upon units and
dimension.

A more useful quantity is the logarithm of volume.

\begin{definition}{Reachability Entropy}{reachability-entropy}
The reachability entropy is

\[
S_R
=

\log\Vol_R.
\]
\end{definition}

Reachability entropy measures the effective number of
future possibilities.

\begin{prop}
If

\[
R_1
\]

and

\[
R_2
\]

are independent reachable regions, then

\[
S_R(R_1\times R_2)
=

S_R(R_1)
+
S_R(R_2).
\]
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Volumes multiply:

\[
\Vol(R_1\times R_2)
=

\Vol(R_1)\Vol(R_2).
\]

Taking logarithms yields

\[
\log
\Vol(R_1\times R_2)
=

\log\Vol(R_1)
+
\log\Vol(R_2).
\]
\end{proof}

This additivity property mirrors the behavior of entropy
throughout statistical mechanics and information theory.

\subsection{Reachability as Distinction Capacity}

Reachability may also be interpreted as a distinction
measure.

Let

\[
\epsilon
\]

denote observational resolution.

The number of distinguishable future states is

\[
N_R
\approx
\frac{\Vol_R}{\epsilon^n}.
\]

The corresponding distinction capacity is

\[
D_R
=

\log N_R.
\]

Substituting gives

\[
D_R
=

## \log\Vol_R

n\log\epsilon.
\]

Hence

\[
D_R
=

S_R
+
\text{constant}.
\]

\begin{prop}
Reachability entropy and future distinction capacity are
equivalent up to scale.
\end{prop}

This result provides a direct bridge between reachability
theory and the distinction framework developed in earlier
chapters.

\subsection{Boundary Structure}

The topology of the reachable set often matters as much as
its size.

\begin{definition}{Reachability Boundary}{reachability-boundary}
The reachability boundary is

\[
\partial R_t.
\]
\end{definition}

Boundary points represent states that are barely
reachable.

Small perturbations may render them inaccessible.

\begin{definition}{Reachability Margin}{reachability-margin}
For state

\[
x\in R_t,
\]

the reachability margin is

\[
m(x)
=

d(x,\partial R_t).
\]
\end{definition}

Small margins indicate fragility.

Large margins indicate robust accessibility.

\subsection{Boundary Collapse}

One of the most important phenomena discussed throughout
the book is boundary collapse.

\begin{definition}{Boundary Collapse}{boundary-collapse}
Boundary collapse occurs when

\[
\Vol_R(t)
\rightarrow0.
\]
\end{definition}

Equivalently,

\[
S_R
\rightarrow
-\infty.
\]

The system retains fewer and fewer future options until
only a negligible set remains.

\begin{theorem}{Reachability Collapse Criterion}{reachability-collapse}
If

\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}
\Vol_R(t)
=

0,
\]

then

\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}
S_R
=

-\infty.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since

\[
S_R
=

\log\Vol_R,
\]

the result follows from

\[
\log x
\rightarrow
-\infty
\]

as

\[
x\rightarrow0^+.
\]
\end{proof}

This simple observation underlies much of the geometric
analysis developed later in the text.

Collapse is often more accurately understood as
reachability contraction than as immediate failure.

\subsection{Reachability and Intelligence}

Throughout Chapters~16--18 intelligence is interpreted in
terms of reachability.

An intelligent system is not merely one that predicts.

It is one that expands accessible futures.

\begin{definition}{Reachability Intelligence}{reachability-intelligence}
The reachability intelligence of a system

\[
\Sigma
\]

is

\[
\mathcal I_R(\Sigma)
=

\frac{d}{dt}
\Vol_R(\Sigma).
\]
\end{definition}

Positive values indicate future expansion.

Negative values indicate future contraction.

\begin{rem}
This definition generalizes many traditional notions of
adaptation, planning, and problem solving.
\end{rem}

\subsection{Reachability and Repair}

Repair can be interpreted geometrically as restoration of
lost reachability.

Suppose damage changes

\[
R_t
\]

to

\[
R_t'.
\]

The reachability deficit is

\[
\Delta_R
=

## \Vol_R

\Vol_R'.
\]

\begin{definition}{Repair Efficiency}{repair-efficiency}
Repair efficiency is

\[
\eta_R
=

\frac{\Delta_R^{\mathrm{restored}}}
{\Delta_R}.
\]
\end{definition}

Perfect repair satisfies

\[
\eta_R=1.
\]

Complete failure satisfies

\[
\eta_R=0.
\]

\subsection{Reachability and Regeneration}

Repair restores lost volume.

Regeneration restores the ability to generate volume.

Let

\[
G_R
=

\frac{d}{dt}
\Vol_R.
\]

\begin{definition}{Regenerative Capacity}{regenerative-capacity}
The regenerative capacity is

\[
\kappa_R
=

\frac{dG_R}{dt}.
\]
\end{definition}

Positive values indicate accelerating expansion of future
possibility.

Negative values indicate declining future flexibility.

This distinction parallels the difference between repair
and regeneration introduced earlier.

\subsection{Reachability and Admissibility}

Reachability alone is insufficient.

Many futures may be reachable while remaining destructive,
pathological, or self-terminating.

Let

\[
\mathcal A
\]

denote the admissible region.

Define admissible reachability volume

\[
\Vol_A
=

\mu(R_t\cap\mathcal A).
\]

\begin{definition}{Admissible Reachability}{adm-reachability}
The admissible reachability entropy is

\[
S_A
=

\log\Vol_A.
\]
\end{definition}

This quantity measures not merely what can happen, but
what can happen while remaining viable.

Much of the later admissibility program can be viewed as
the study of

\[
\Vol_A
\]

rather than

\[
\Vol_R.
\]

\subsection{The Reachability Principle}

One of the recurring themes of the text may be stated
formally.

\begin{theorem}{Reachability Principle}{reachability-principle}
A system possessing greater admissible reachability
contains a larger space of future distinctions.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Admissible reachability volume determines the number of
accessible distinguishable future states.

Larger admissible volume therefore implies greater future
distinction capacity.
\end{proof}

This theorem forms one of the conceptual bridges between
distinction theory and admissibility geometry.

\subsection{Summary}

Reachability theory transforms dynamics into geometry.
Reachable sets describe accessible futures, reachability
volume measures future flexibility, reachability entropy
measures future distinction capacity, and boundary
collapse corresponds to contraction of possibility space.

Repair restores lost reachability. Regeneration restores
the ability to generate reachability. Intelligence
expands reachability. Admissibility restricts
reachability to viable futures.

For this reason reachability volume serves as one of the
central geometric quantities appearing throughout
Chapters~13--31 and provides much of the mathematical
foundation for the admissibility program.

\section{Admissibility Geometry}

The central claim of this book is that persistence alone
is not sufficient.

A crystal persists.

A bureaucracy persists.

A parasite persists.

A dictatorship persists.

A cancer persists.

Persistence therefore cannot serve as a universal measure
of value, viability, intelligence, or success.

The deeper question concerns the preservation of future
possibility.

Not every continuation preserves the conditions required
for further continuation.

A system may survive while progressively destroying the
space of futures available to itself and others.

This observation motivates the concept of admissibility.

Admissibility concerns the geometry of viable future
possibility.

The primary object of study is not a state, a trajectory,
or a utility function.

It is the structure of futures that remain available.

\subsection{The Admissible Region}

Let

\[
X
\]

denote a state space, trajectory space, semantic space,
or possibility space.

\begin{definition}{Admissible Region}{adm-region}
The admissible region is a subset

\[
\mathcal A
\subseteq
X
\]

consisting of all states satisfying the viability
conditions of the system.
\end{definition}

The precise definition of admissibility depends upon the
domain.

In ecology it may correspond to sustainability.

In engineering it may correspond to safety.

In governance it may correspond to preservation of future
optionality.

In scientific inquiry it may correspond to preservation
of revision and falsifiability.

The mathematical structure remains the same.

\begin{rem}
Admissibility is intentionally more general than
optimization.

Optimization asks whether a state is preferred.

Admissibility asks whether future preference remains
possible.
\end{rem}

\subsection{Admissibility Volume}

The simplest global quantity associated with an
admissible region is its measure.

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Volume}{adm-volume}
Let

\[
\mu
\]

be a measure on (X).

The admissibility volume is

\[
V_A
=

\mu(\mathcal A).
\]
\end{definition}

Admissibility volume measures the size of the viable
future region.

Large values correspond to broad flexibility.

Small values correspond to fragility.

Vanishing admissibility volume corresponds to collapse.

\begin{prop}
If

\[
\mathcal A_1
\subseteq
\mathcal A_2,
\]

then

\[
V_A(\mathcal A_1)
\le
V_A(\mathcal A_2).
\]
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Monotonicity follows directly from the monotonicity of
measures.

If one set is contained within another, its measure
cannot exceed the measure of the larger set.
\end{proof}

This simple observation provides the basis for much of
the ordering structure used throughout later chapters.

\subsection{Admissibility Entropy}

Volume alone is often inconvenient because it depends upon
dimension and units.

As in statistical mechanics and reachability theory, it
is useful to introduce a logarithmic quantity.

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Entropy}{adm-entropy}
The admissibility entropy is

\[
S_A
=

\log V_A.
\]
\end{definition}

Admissibility entropy measures the effective number of
admissible futures.

\begin{prop}
For independent admissible systems

\[
\mathcal A_1
\]

and

\[
\mathcal A_2,
\]

\[
S_A(\mathcal A_1\times\mathcal A_2)
=

S_A(\mathcal A_1)
+
S_A(\mathcal A_2).
\]
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Since volume is multiplicative,

\[
V_A(\mathcal A_1\times\mathcal A_2)
=

V_A(\mathcal A_1)
V_A(\mathcal A_2).
\]

Taking logarithms gives the result.
\end{proof}

This additive property makes admissibility entropy
analogous to thermodynamic entropy and reachability
entropy.

\subsection{Local Admissibility Structure}

Global volume is only part of the story.

The local geometry of admissibility is equally important.

Let

\[
x\in\mathcal A.
\]

Small perturbations may either preserve or destroy
admissibility.

To quantify this sensitivity we introduce an
admissibility potential.

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Potential}{adm-potential}
The admissibility potential is

\[
\Psi(x)
=

-\log V_A(x),
\]

where

\[
V_A(x)
\]

denotes the local admissible volume associated with the
neighborhood of (x).
\end{definition}

Large values of

\[
\Psi
\]

correspond to regions where admissible futures are scarce.

Small values correspond to regions possessing abundant
future flexibility.

\subsection{Admissibility Curvature}

Curvature measures how rapidly admissibility changes.

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Curvature Tensor}{adm-curvature-tensor}
The admissibility curvature tensor is

\[
K_{ij}
=

\nabla_i\nabla_j\Psi.
\]
\end{definition}

Equivalently,

\[
K_{ij}
=

*

\nabla_i\nabla_j
\log V_A.
\]

The tensor

\[
K_{ij}
\]

is the Hessian of the admissibility potential.

It measures second-order sensitivity of future
possibility.

\begin{definition}{Scalar Admissibility Curvature}{scalar-adm-curvature}
The scalar admissibility curvature is

\[
K_A
=

g^{ij}K_{ij}.
\]
\end{definition}

\subsection{Interpretation of Curvature}

The sign of admissibility curvature carries geometric
meaning.

\begin{prop}
Positive admissibility curvature corresponds to local
contraction of future possibility.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
If

\[
K_{ij}
=

\nabla_i\nabla_j\Psi
\]

is positive semidefinite, then

\[
\Psi
\]

is locally convex.

Since

\[
\Psi
=

-\log V_A,
\]

convexity of

\[
\Psi
\]

corresponds to local concavity of admissible volume.

Hence nearby perturbations reduce admissible volume.
\end{proof}

\begin{prop}
Negative admissibility curvature corresponds to local
expansion of future possibility.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
If

\[
K_{ij}
\]

is negative semidefinite then

\[
\Psi
\]

is locally concave.

Therefore

\[
V_A
\]

is locally expanding.

Small perturbations increase future possibility.
\end{proof}

Positive curvature therefore signals fragility.

Negative curvature signals generativity.

\subsection{Boundary Geometry}

The topology of admissibility is controlled by its
boundary.

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Boundary}{adm-boundary}
The admissibility boundary is

\[
\partial\mathcal A.
\]
\end{definition}

Points near

\[
\partial\mathcal A
\]

possess heightened sensitivity.

Small perturbations may move trajectories into
inadmissible regions.

\begin{definition}{Boundary Distance}{boundary-distance}
The boundary distance of state

\[
x
\]

is

\[
d_A(x)
=

d(x,\partial\mathcal A).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{prop}
Boundary distance provides a first-order measure of local
admissibility robustness.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
For sufficiently small perturbations

\[
|\delta x|
<
d_A(x),
\]

the perturbed state remains inside

\[
\mathcal A.
\]

Hence larger boundary distances imply larger admissible
perturbation neighborhoods.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Admissibility Gradient}

The gradient of admissibility volume determines the
direction of maximal expansion.

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Gradient}{adm-gradient}
The admissibility gradient is

\[
\nabla V_A.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{prop}
The direction

\[
\nabla V_A
\]

maximizes local increase in admissible volume.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
For a differentiable scalar field the gradient points in
the direction of greatest increase.

Since

\[
V_A
\]

is a scalar field, the result follows directly from
multivariable calculus.
\end{proof}

The admissibility gradient therefore provides a natural
direction of generative motion.

\subsection{Admissible Flows}

Let

\[
\phi_t
\]

be a flow on the state space.

\begin{definition}{Admissible Flow}{adm-flow}
A flow is admissible if

\[
\phi_t(\mathcal A)
\subseteq
\mathcal A
\]

for all

\[
t\ge0.
\]
\end{definition}

Admissible flows preserve viability.

Many systems satisfy this condition only approximately.

Consequently admissibility must often be treated as a
stability problem.

\subsection{Generative Admissibility}

The central geometric principle of the framework is not
mere preservation but expansion.

\begin{definition}{Generative Admissibility}{generative-adm}
A system is generatively admissible if

\[
\frac{dV_A}{dt}
\ge
0.
\]
\end{definition}

This condition states that admissible volume must remain
constant or increase through time.

\begin{theorem}{Generative Admissibility Theorem}{generative-adm-theorem}
If

\[
\frac{dV_A}{dt}
\ge0,
\]

then admissibility entropy satisfies

\[
\frac{dS_A}{dt}
\ge0.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since

\[
S_A
=

\log V_A,
\]

differentiation yields

\[
\frac{dS_A}{dt}
=

\frac1{V_A}
\frac{dV_A}{dt}.
\]

Because

\[
V_A>0
\]

and

\[
\frac{dV_A}{dt}\ge0,
\]

it follows that

\[
\frac{dS_A}{dt}
\ge0.
\]
\end{proof}

Thus generative admissibility corresponds to monotonic
growth of admissible future possibility.

\subsection{The Collapse Condition}

The opposite regime is equally important.

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Collapse}{adm-collapse}
Admissibility collapse occurs when

\[
V_A
\rightarrow0.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Collapse Theorem}{collapse-theorem}
If

\[
V_A(t)
\rightarrow0,
\]

then

\[
S_A(t)
\rightarrow-\infty.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since

\[
S_A
=

\log V_A,
\]

the result follows from

\[
\log x
\rightarrow
-\infty
\]

as

\[
x
\rightarrow
0^+.
\]
\end{proof}

Admissibility collapse therefore represents complete
destruction of future viable possibility.

\subsection{Admissibility and Reachability}

Reachability and admissibility are closely related but not
identical.

Reachability asks:

\[
\text{What futures can be reached?}
\]

Admissibility asks:

\[
\text{What futures should remain reachable?}
\]

Define admissible reachability volume

\[
V_{AR}
=

\mu(R_t\cap\mathcal A).
\]

The corresponding entropy is

\[
S_{AR}
=

\log V_{AR}.
\]

This quantity measures viable future possibility.

Much of the framework developed in Chapters~13--31 can be
interpreted as the study of

\[
V_{AR}
\]

rather than raw reachability.

\subsection{Admissibility and Intelligence}

A recurring theme of the text is that intelligence should
not be measured solely by optimization success.

A sufficiently powerful optimizer can maximize an
objective while simultaneously reducing future
possibility.

Such systems remain effective but become
inadmissible.

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Intelligence}{adm-intelligence}
The admissibility intelligence of a system is

\[
\mathcal I_A
=

\frac{dV_A}{dt}.
\]
\end{definition}

Positive values indicate expansion of viable futures.

Negative values indicate contraction.

This interpretation provides a bridge between
intelligence, regeneration, and future preservation.

\subsection{Fundamental Principle}

The preceding constructions culminate in a single
geometric statement.

\begin{theorem}{Fundamental Admissibility Principle}{fund-adm-principle}
A transformation is generatively admissible if and only if
it preserves or expands admissible volume.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By definition, generative admissibility requires

\[
\frac{dV_A}{dt}
\ge0.
\]

This condition is precisely preservation or expansion of
admissible volume.

The equivalence therefore follows immediately.
\end{proof}

This theorem functions throughout the remainder of the
book in a role analogous to the Second Law of
thermodynamics.

The Second Law constrains entropy.

The Fundamental Admissibility Principle constrains viable
future possibility.

\subsection{Summary}

Admissibility geometry studies the shape of viable future
possibility. The admissible region defines the space of
acceptable futures, admissibility volume measures its
size, admissibility entropy measures its logarithmic
capacity, admissibility curvature measures local
contraction or expansion, and admissibility boundaries
separate viable futures from collapse.

The central quantity is admissibility volume

\[
V_A.
\]

The central condition is

\[
\frac{dV_A}{dt}\ge0.
\]

This condition provides a geometric foundation 
and serves as the unifying principle linking
repair, regeneration, reachability, intelligence,
governance, ecology, and future preservation.

\section{Dependency Structure of Core Quantities}

One of the central claims of this text is that many
concepts commonly treated as independent are in fact
derived from earlier structures. The framework developed
throughout the book can therefore be organized as a
hierarchy of mathematical dependencies.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{longtable}{p{3cm}p{4cm}p{7cm}}
\toprule
Quantity & Definition Source & Interpretation \\
\midrule
\endhead

$D$
&
Primitive
&
Distinction measure; count of distinguishable classes.
\\

$H$
&
$D$
&
Entropy; hidden multiplicity beneath distinctions.
\\

$\hist$
&
$H$
&
History space generated by evolving distinction states.
\\

$\reco$
&
$\hist$
&
Recoverability; ability to reconstruct prior states from
histories.
\\

$\repair$
&
$\reco$
&
Repair operator acting to restore lost distinctions.
\\

$\kappa$
&
$\repair$
&
Repair capacity.
\\

$\Vol_R$
&
$\repair,\kappa$
&
Reachable future volume.
\\

$S_R$
&
$\Vol_R$
&
Reachability entropy.
\\

$\mathcal A$
&
$\Vol_R$
&
Admissible region of reachable states.
\\

$V_A$
&
$\mathcal A$
&
Admissibility volume.
\\

$S_A$
&
$V_A$
&
Admissibility entropy.
\\

$\Psi$
&
$V_A$
&
Admissibility potential.
\\

$K_A$
&
$\Psi$
&
Admissibility curvature.
\\

$\mathcal I_R$
&
$\Vol_R$
&
Reachability intelligence.
\\

$\mathcal I_A$
&
$V_A$
&
Admissibility intelligence.
\\

$\mathbf I_{\mathcal A}$
&
All preceding quantities
&
Full admissibility invariant.
\\

\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\section{Master Dependency Chain}

The principal conceptual dependency chain developed
throughout the text may be summarized as

\[
D
\;\Longrightarrow\;
H
\;\Longrightarrow\;
\hist
\;\Longrightarrow\;
\reco
\;\Longrightarrow\;
\repair
\;\Longrightarrow\;
\Vol_R
\;\Longrightarrow\;
\mathcal A
\;\Longrightarrow\;
V_A
\;\Longrightarrow\;
K_A.
\]

The interpretation of this chain is as follows.

Distinctions generate information.
Information generates histories.
Histories permit recoverability.
Recoverability makes repair possible.
Repair preserves reachability.
Reachability determines admissibility.
Admissibility determines future possibility.
The geometry of future possibility is encoded in
admissibility curvature.

\begin{proposition}[Hierarchy of Failure]{hierarchy-failure}
Failure at any level propagates upward through the
dependency chain.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If distinctions cannot be maintained, information cannot
be preserved. Without preserved information, histories
cannot be reconstructed. Without recoverable histories,
repair becomes impossible. Without repair, reachable
volume contracts. As reachable volume contracts,
admissible volume contracts. Consequently admissibility
curvature becomes increasingly positive, signalling
collapse of future possibility.

Therefore degradation at any lower level necessarily
appears as admissibility loss at higher levels.
\end{proof}

\\

% ============================================================
%  APPENDIX B: COMPUTATIONAL TOOLS
% ============================================================
\chapter{Computational Tools}
\label{app:comp}
\section{Computation as Distinction Transformation}

Let

\[
X
\]

be a state space and let

\[
\mathcal P(X)
\]

denote the set of partitions of \(X\).

A distinction structure is a partition

\[
\pi
=
\{P_1,\ldots,P_n\}
\]

with

\[
P_i\cap P_j=\varnothing
\]

for

\[
i\neq j
\]

and

\[
\bigcup_i P_i=X.
\]

Traditional computation is represented as a sequence of
state transitions

\[
x_0
\rightarrow
x_1
\rightarrow
x_2
\rightarrow
\cdots.
\]

We instead consider the induced sequence of partitions

\[
\pi_0
\rightarrow
\pi_1
\rightarrow
\pi_2
\rightarrow
\cdots.
\]

The computational object is therefore not a state but a
trajectory through distinction space.

\begin{definition}[Distinction State]{distinction-state}
A distinction state is an element

\[
d
\in
\mathcal P(X).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Distinction Dynamics]{distinction-dynamics}
A distinction dynamics is a mapping

\[
F :
\mathcal P(X)
\rightarrow
\mathcal P(X).
\]
\end{definition}

The induced computational trajectory is

\[
d_{t+1}
=
F(d_t).
\]

\begin{definition}[Distinction Capacity]{distinction-capacity}
For partition

\[
d
=
\{P_1,\ldots,P_n\}
\]

define

\[
D(d)
=
\log n.
\]
\end{definition}

The quantity

\[
D(d)
\]

measures the number of distinguishable classes available
to the computation.

\begin{prop}
Let

\[
d_1
\preceq
d_2
\]

denote refinement.

Then

\[
D(d_1)
\le
D(d_2).
\]
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Refinement increases the number of partition cells.

Hence

\[
|d_1|
\le
|d_2|.
\]

Applying monotonicity of the logarithm gives

\[
\log|d_1|
\le
\log|d_2|.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Computation–Distinction Equivalence]{comp-distinction-equivalence}
Every deterministic computation

\[
C:X\rightarrow X
\]

induces a distinction dynamics

\[
F_C:\mathcal P(X)\rightarrow\mathcal P(X).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let

\[
\pi
=
\{P_1,\ldots,P_n\}.
\]

Define

\[
F_C(\pi)
=
\{
C(P_1),
\ldots,
C(P_n)
\}.
\]

The image of each partition cell remains a partition of
the reachable image of the state space.

Hence

\[
F_C
\]

acts on distinction structures directly.
\end{proof}

\section{Flow Computing}

A flow system is a directed acyclic graph $G = (V,E)$
where vertices are transformations and edges transport
distinctions.
The computational state at time $t$ is
$X_t = \bigcup_{v \in V_t}\mathrm{Output}(v)$.

\begin{definition}[Distinction Transport Operator]{distinction-transport-operator}
Let

\[
G=(V,E)
\]

be a flow graph.

Define

\[
T_G :
\mathcal D
\rightarrow
\mathcal D
\]

by

\[
T_G(d)
=
\bigcup_{e\in E}
T_e(d)
\]

where

\[
T_e
\]

is the distinction transport induced by edge \(e\).
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Flow Equivalence Theorem]{flow-equivalence}
Two flow systems

\[
G_1
\]

and

\[
G_2
\]

are observationally equivalent if and only if

\[
T_{G_1}
=
T_{G_2}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Assume

\[
T_{G_1}
=
T_{G_2}.
\]

For every initial distinction structure

\[
d
\]

both systems generate identical transported
distinctions.

Therefore every observable distinction available to an
observer is identical.

Hence observations coincide.

Conversely, suppose

\[
T_{G_1}
\neq
T_{G_2}.
\]

Then there exists

\[
d
\]

such that

\[
T_{G_1}(d)
\neq
T_{G_2}(d).
\]

Therefore at least one distinction differs between the
two systems.

An observer capable of accessing that distinction can
separate the systems.

Hence they are not observationally equivalent.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}{Flow Equivalence Theorem}{flow-equiv}
  Two computational systems are observationally equivalent
  iff they induce identical distinction transport operators.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Observations depend only upon distinctions available
  to an observer. Identical transport operators imply
  identical reachable distinctions. Any change in
  transport changes at least one reachable distinction.
\end{proof}

Define transport entropy $S_F = -\sum_i p_i\log p_i$
where $p_i = f_i / \sum_j f_j$ and $f_i$ is edge flow.

\begin{theorem}{Flow Compression Theorem}{flow-compression}
  Maximum compression occurs when transport entropy is
  minimised subject to reachability constraints.
\end{theorem}

\section{Spherepop Formal Semantics}

A Spherepop configuration is $\Sigma = (H, S)$
where $H$ is history and $S$ is scope.
The transition relation $\Sigma \to \Sigma'$:

\medskip
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Rule & Pre-state & Post-state \\
\midrule
$\mathbf{Pop}(e)$      & $(H \cup \{e\}, S)$ & $(H, S)$ \\
$\mathbf{Refuse}(e)$   & $(H, S \cup \{e\})$ & $(H, S)$ + witness \\
$\mathbf{Bind}(n,v)$   & $(H, S)$ & $(H, S \cup \{n \mapsto v\})$ \\
$\mathbf{Collapse}(\pi)$ & $(H, S)$ & $(H, \pi(S))$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{theorem}{History Monotonicity}{app-history-mono}
  For any valid Spherepop computation,
  $H_t \sqsubseteq H_{t+1}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  No operation removes historical events.
  Pop, Refuse, Bind, and Collapse each append to $H$
  or leave it unchanged.
\end{proof}

Define collapse quotient $Q_C = |S| / |\pi(S)|$.

\begin{theorem}{Collapse Bound}{collapse-bound}
  For finite scope, $Q_C \ge 1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}[History Poset]{history-poset}
Let

\[
(H,\sqsubseteq)
\]

be the partially ordered set of histories where

\[
H_1
\sqsubseteq
H_2
\]

iff

\[
H_1
\subseteq
H_2.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[History Monotonicity]{history-monotonicity}
For every valid Spherepop execution

\[
H_t
\sqsubseteq
H_{t+1}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Consider each transition rule.

Pop removes an active scope element but appends an event
to history.

Refuse appends a refusal witness.

Bind appends a binding event.

Collapse appends a projection event.

No rule removes historical records.

Therefore

\[
H_t
\subseteq
H_{t+1}.
\]

Hence

\[
H_t
\sqsubseteq
H_{t+1}.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
Spherepop histories form a monotone chain in the history
lattice.
\end{corollary}

\section{HYDRA State Transitions}

HYDRA state space: $M = \prod_{i=1}^k M_i$.
Module dynamics: $\dot{M}_i = F_i(M)$.
Disagreement energy: $E_D = \sum_{i<j} d(M_i, M_j)^2$.

\begin{theorem}{HYDRA Convergence Theorem}{hydra-conv}
  If $\dot{E}_D < 0$ for all non-equilibrium states,
  module consensus is globally asymptotically stable.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  $E_D$ is a positive-definite Lyapunov function with
  negative-definite derivative. Standard Lyapunov theory
  implies convergence.
\end{proof}
\section{TARTAN Recursive Distinction}

Let \(M\) be a semantic manifold equipped with a measure
\(\mu\). A TARTAN structure is a recursively refined
partition of \(M\). At depth \(0\), let

\[
\mathcal T_0=\{T_1,\ldots,T_m\}
\]

be an initial measurable partition. Recursive subdivision
produces partitions

\[
\mathcal T_0 \preceq \mathcal T_1 \preceq
\mathcal T_2 \preceq \cdots
\]

where each tile satisfies

\[
T_i=\bigcup_j T_{ij},
\qquad
T_{ij}\cap T_{ik}=\varnothing
\quad(j\neq k).
\]

The distinction capacity at depth \(n\) is

\[
D_n=\log |\mathcal T_n|.
\]

More generally, if the tiles carry weights \(p_i^{(n)}\),
the weighted distinction capacity is

\[
D_n
=
-\sum_{T_i\in\mathcal T_n}
p_i^{(n)}
\log p_i^{(n)}.
\]

\begin{definition}{Recursive Distinction Operator}{tartan-recursive-op}
  A recursive distinction operator is a map
  \[
    \mathcal R:\mathcal T_n\to \mathcal T_{n+1}
  \]
  such that every tile \(T\in\mathcal T_n\) is replaced by
  a finite measurable partition
  \[
    \mathcal R(T)=\{T_1,\ldots,T_{k(T)}\}
  \]
  with
  \[
    T=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{k(T)}T_\ell,
    \qquad
    T_\ell\cap T_m=\varnothing
    \quad(\ell\neq m).
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}{Subdivision Factor}{tartan-subdivision-factor}
  The subdivision factor at depth \(n\) is
  \[
    \lambda_n
=
    \frac{|\mathcal T_{n+1}|}{|\mathcal T_n|}.
  \]
  If \(\lambda_n=\lambda\) for all \(n\), the TARTAN
  refinement is called homogeneous.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Recursive Distinction Theorem}{recursive-dist}
  If each subdivision increases distinguishability by a
  constant factor \(\lambda>1\), then after depth \(n\)
  the number of distinguishable tiles satisfies
  \[
    |\mathcal T_n|
=
    \lambda^n|\mathcal T_0|.
  \]
  Consequently the unweighted distinction capacity satisfies
  \[
    D_n
=
    D_0+n\log\lambda.
  \]
  If instead \(D_n\) denotes raw distinction count rather
  than logarithmic capacity, then
  \[
    D_n=\lambda^nD_0.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By definition of homogeneous subdivision,
  \[
    |\mathcal T_{n+1}|
=
    \lambda|\mathcal T_n|.
  \]
  Iterating gives
  \[
    |\mathcal T_n|
=
    \lambda^n|\mathcal T_0|.
  \]
  The logarithmic distinction capacity is therefore
  \[
    D_n
=
    \log|\mathcal T_n|
=
    \log(\lambda^n|\mathcal T_0|)
=
    n\log\lambda+\log|\mathcal T_0|
=
    D_0+n\log\lambda.
  \]
  If \(D_n\) is used for the raw count
  \( |\mathcal T_n| \), the exponential formula follows
  directly.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}{Distinction Density}{dist-density}
  Let \(\rho:M\to\mathbb R_{\ge0}\) denote local
  distinction density. For a measurable region \(U\subseteq M\),
  the local distinction mass is
  \[
    D(U)=\int_U \rho(x)\,d\mu(x).
  \]
\end{definition}

The semantic curvature associated with \(\rho\) is

\[
K_S
=
-\nabla^2\log\rho.
\]

In local coordinates this is the tensor

\[
(K_S)_{ij}
=
-\nabla_i\nabla_j\log\rho.
\]

Its trace is

\[
\operatorname{tr}(K_S)
=
-g^{ij}\nabla_i\nabla_j\log\rho
=
-\Delta\log\rho.
\]

Positive semantic curvature corresponds to local
concentration of distinctions and possible bottlenecking;
negative semantic curvature corresponds to local spreading
and semantic expansion.

\subsection{Lyapunov Formulation}

Let \(\rho(x,t)\) evolve by a refinement--diffusion
equation

\[
\frac{\partial \rho}{\partial t}
=
\alpha \Delta\rho
+
\beta R(\rho),
\]

where \(\alpha\ge0\) controls smoothing and \(\beta\ge0\)
controls recursive refinement. A simple stabilizing model
is

\[
R(\rho)=\rho\left(1-\frac{\rho}{\rho_*}\right),
\]

so that

\[
\frac{\partial \rho}{\partial t}
=
\alpha\Delta\rho
+
\beta\rho\left(1-\frac{\rho}{\rho_*}\right).
\]

Here \(\rho_*>0\) is the target distinction density.

Define the Lyapunov functional

\[
\mathcal L[\rho]
=
\frac12
\int_M
(\rho-\rho_*)^2
\,d\mu.
\]

Then

\[
\frac{d\mathcal L}{dt}
=
\int_M
(\rho-\rho_*)
\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}
\,d\mu.
\]

Substituting the dynamics gives

\[
\frac{d\mathcal L}{dt}
=
\alpha
\int_M
(\rho-\rho_*)\Delta\rho
\,d\mu
+
\beta
\int_M
(\rho-\rho_*)
\rho\left(1-\frac{\rho}{\rho_*}\right)
\,d\mu.
\]

Assume either compact \(M\) without boundary or boundary
conditions eliminating boundary terms. Integration by parts
yields

\[
\int_M
(\rho-\rho_*)\Delta\rho
\,d\mu
=
-\int_M
\|\nabla\rho\|^2
\,d\mu.
\]

For the reaction term, observe that

\[
\rho\left(1-\frac{\rho}{\rho_*}\right)
=
\frac{\rho}{\rho_*}(\rho_*-\rho)
=
-\frac{\rho}{\rho_*}(\rho-\rho_*).
\]

Hence

\[
(\rho-\rho_*)
\rho\left(1-\frac{\rho}{\rho_*}\right)
=
-\frac{\rho}{\rho_*}(\rho-\rho_*)^2.
\]

Therefore

\[
\frac{d\mathcal L}{dt}
=
-\alpha
\int_M
\|\nabla\rho\|^2
\,d\mu
-
\beta
\int_M
\frac{\rho}{\rho_*}
(\rho-\rho_*)^2
\,d\mu.
\]

Since \(\alpha,\beta,\rho,\rho_*\ge0\), both terms are
nonpositive:

\[
\frac{d\mathcal L}{dt}\le0.
\]

\begin{theorem}{TARTAN Lyapunov Stability}{tartan-lyapunov}
  Under compact or no-flux boundary conditions, the target
  distinction density \(\rho_*\) is Lyapunov stable for
  the refinement--diffusion dynamics
  \[
    \frac{\partial \rho}{\partial t}
=
    \alpha\Delta\rho
    +
    \beta\rho\left(1-\frac{\rho}{\rho_*}\right).
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  The functional
  \[
    \mathcal L[\rho]
=
    \frac12
    \int_M
    (\rho-\rho_*)^2
    \,d\mu
  \]
  satisfies \(\mathcal L[\rho]\ge0\), with equality iff
  \(\rho=\rho_*\) almost everywhere. The calculation above
  gives
  \[
    \frac{d\mathcal L}{dt}
=
    -\alpha
    \int_M
    \|\nabla\rho\|^2
    \,d\mu
-
    \beta
    \int_M
    \frac{\rho}{\rho_*}
    (\rho-\rho_*)^2
    \,d\mu
    \le0.
  \]
  Hence \(\mathcal L\) is nonincreasing along trajectories.
  Therefore \(\rho_*\) is Lyapunov stable.
\end{proof}

If \(\alpha>0\), \(\beta>0\), and \(\rho>0\) almost
everywhere, then equality

\[
\frac{d\mathcal L}{dt}=0
\]

requires

\[
\nabla\rho=0
\]

and

\[
\rho=\rho_*.
\]

Thus the equilibrium is asymptotically stable modulo
measure-zero degeneracies.

\begin{corollary}{Asymptotic Stabilization}{tartan-asymptotic}
  If \(\alpha>0\), \(\beta>0\), and \(\rho(x,t)>0\) almost
  everywhere, then \(\rho(t)\to\rho_*\) in \(L^2(M)\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  The Lyapunov functional is bounded below and
  nonincreasing, hence convergent. Its derivative vanishes
  only at the invariant set \(\rho=\rho_*\). By LaSalle's
  invariance principle, trajectories converge to that set.
\end{proof}

\section{MEM|8 Memory Architecture}

Let \(X\) be a cue space, event space, or semantic state
space equipped with a measure \(\mu\). Stored events are
represented as points or structured elements

\[
e_i\in X,
\qquad
i=1,\ldots,N.
\]

Each event carries a weight

\[
w_i\ge0,
\]

representing salience, reinforcement, recency, frequency,
or repair relevance.

Let

\[
K:X\times X\rightarrow\mathbb R_{\ge0}
\]

be a similarity kernel. The memory field generated by the
stored events is

\[
M(x,t)
=
\sum_{i=1}^{N}
w_i(t)K(x,e_i).
\]

The value \(M(x,t)\) measures the activation of stored
events under cue \(x\) at time \(t\).

\begin{definition}{Memory Kernel}{mem-kernel}
  A memory kernel is a function
  \[
    K:X\times X\to\mathbb R_{\ge0}
  \]
  satisfying
  \[
    K(x,e)\ge0
  \]
  and usually
  \[
    K(x,e)=K(e,x).
  \]
  If \(K\) is positive definite, then for any finite set
  \(\{x_1,\ldots,x_N\}\subset X\) and coefficients
  \(a_i\in\mathbb R\),
  \[
    \sum_{i,j=1}^N a_i a_j K(x_i,x_j)\ge0.
  \]
\end{definition}

A common example is the Gaussian kernel

\[
K(x,e)
=
\exp
\left(
-\frac{d(x,e)^2}{2\sigma^2}
\right),
\]

where \(\sigma>0\) controls the radius of association.

Small \(\sigma\) produces sharply localized recall.

Large \(\sigma\) produces broad associative recall.

\begin{definition}{Memory Field}{mem-field}
  Given events \(e_i\), weights \(w_i(t)\), and kernel
  \(K\), the memory field is
  \[
    M(x,t)
=
    \sum_{i=1}^{N}
    w_i(t)K(x,e_i).
  \]
\end{definition}

\subsection{Ecphory}

Ecphory is the activation of a stored memory by a cue. In
this formalization, recall occurs when the memory field
crosses a threshold.

Let

\[
\theta>0
\]

be a recall threshold.

\begin{definition}{Ecphoric Region}{ecphoric-region}
  The ecphoric region at time \(t\) is
  \[
    E_\theta(t)
=
    \{x\in X:M(x,t)>\theta\}.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}{Recall Indicator}{recall-indicator}
  The recall indicator is
  \[
    \chi_\theta(x,t)
=
    \begin{cases}
      1, & M(x,t)>\theta,\\
      0, & M(x,t)\le\theta.
    \end{cases}
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Ecphory Threshold Theorem}{ecphory}
  Recall occurs at cue \(x\) and time \(t\) if and only if
  \[
    \sum_{i=1}^{N} w_i(t)K(x,e_i)>\theta.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By definition, recall occurs iff
  \[
    M(x,t)>\theta.
  \]
  Substituting the definition of \(M\) gives
  \[
    \sum_{i=1}^{N} w_i(t)K(x,e_i)>\theta.
  \]
  Hence the two conditions are equivalent.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Single-Event Recall Radius}

For a single stored event \(e\) with weight \(w\), the
memory field is

\[
M(x)
=
wK(x,e).
\]

For the Gaussian kernel,

\[
M(x)
=
w
\exp
\left(
-\frac{d(x,e)^2}{2\sigma^2}
\right).
\]

Recall occurs iff

\[
w
\exp
\left(
-\frac{d(x,e)^2}{2\sigma^2}
\right)
>
\theta.
\]

Assuming \(w>\theta\), taking logarithms yields

\[
-\frac{d(x,e)^2}{2\sigma^2}
>
\log\left(\frac{\theta}{w}\right).
\]

Thus

\[
d(x,e)^2
<
2\sigma^2
\log\left(\frac{w}{\theta}\right).
\]

\begin{proposition}{Gaussian Recall Radius}{gaussian-recall-radius}
  For a single Gaussian memory trace, recall occurs inside
  the ball
  \[
    d(x,e)
    <
    r_\theta,
  \]
  where
  \[
    r_\theta
=
    \sigma
    \sqrt{
      2\log\left(\frac{w}{\theta}\right)
    }.
  \]
  If \(w\le\theta\), no cue recalls the event.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  The inequality above gives the result directly. If
  \(w\le\theta\), the maximum of the field occurs at
  \(x=e\), where \(M(e)=w\le\theta\), so the threshold is
  never crossed.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Superposition and Associative Recall}

For multiple events, recall can occur even if no single
trace individually crosses threshold.

\begin{proposition}{Associative Summation}{assoc-summation}
  Suppose
  \[
    w_iK(x,e_i)\le\theta
  \]
  for every \(i\), but
  \[
    \sum_{i=1}^N w_iK(x,e_i)>\theta.
  \]
  Then recall occurs by associative summation.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  Recall depends on the total field
  \[
    M(x)=\sum_i w_iK(x,e_i),
  \]
  not on any single summand. Therefore the total may cross
  threshold even when no individual term does.
\end{proof}

This formalizes pattern completion. A cue need not match
one stored event exactly. It may activate a cluster of
nearby traces whose combined field exceeds threshold.

\subsection{Interference}

If many events have overlapping kernels, recall becomes
ambiguous. Define the contribution of event \(e_i\) at cue
\(x\) by

\[
a_i(x,t)
=
w_i(t)K(x,e_i).
\]

The normalized attribution is

\[
\alpha_i(x,t)
=
\frac{a_i(x,t)}
{\sum_j a_j(x,t)}
\]

whenever \(M(x,t)>0\).

\begin{definition}{Recall Ambiguity}{recall-ambiguity}
  Recall ambiguity is the entropy
  \[
    H_{\mathrm{rec}}(x,t)
=
-
    \sum_i
    \alpha_i(x,t)\log\alpha_i(x,t).
  \]
\end{definition}

Low ambiguity means one trace dominates recall.

High ambiguity means many traces contribute comparably.

\begin{proposition}{Dominant Trace Criterion}{dominant-trace}
  If
  \[
    \alpha_k(x,t)>\frac12,
  \]
  then event \(e_k\) contributes more activation than all
  other events combined.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  Since
  \[
    \sum_i\alpha_i=1,
  \]
  the contribution of all events other than \(k\) is
  \[
    1-\alpha_k.
  \]
  If \(\alpha_k>1/2\), then
  \[
    \alpha_k>1-\alpha_k.
  \]
\end{proof}

\subsection{Memory Decay}

Let memory weights decay according to

\[
\frac{dw_i}{dt}
=
-\lambda_i w_i,
\qquad
\lambda_i\ge0.
\]

Then

\[
w_i(t)
=
w_i(0)e^{-\lambda_i t}.
\]

The memory field becomes

\[
M(x,t)
=
\sum_i
w_i(0)e^{-\lambda_i t}K(x,e_i).
\]

\begin{theorem}{Exponential Forgetting}{exp-forgetting}
  If all decay rates satisfy
  \[
    \lambda_i\ge\lambda_{\min}>0,
  \]
  then
  \[
    M(x,t)
    \le
    e^{-\lambda_{\min}t}M(x,0).
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Since
  \[
    e^{-\lambda_i t}\le e^{-\lambda_{\min}t}
  \]
  for all \(i\), and \(w_iK(x,e_i)\ge0\),
  \[
    M(x,t)
=
    \sum_i
    w_i(0)e^{-\lambda_i t}K(x,e_i)
    \le
    e^{-\lambda_{\min}t}
    \sum_i w_i(0)K(x,e_i).
  \]
  The final sum is \(M(x,0)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}{Recall Time Bound}{recall-time-bound}
  If
  \[
    M(x,0)>\theta
  \]
  and all traces decay with common rate \(\lambda\), then
  recall at \(x\) persists until
  \[
    t
    <
    \frac1\lambda
    \log
    \left(
      \frac{M(x,0)}{\theta}
    \right).
  \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  With common decay rate,
  \[
    M(x,t)=e^{-\lambda t}M(x,0).
  \]
  Recall requires
  \[
    e^{-\lambda t}M(x,0)>\theta.
  \]
  Solving gives
  \[
    t<
    \frac1\lambda
    \log
    \left(
      \frac{M(x,0)}{\theta}
    \right).
  \]
\end{proof}

\subsection{Reinforcement}

Memory traces may also be reinforced. Let cue exposure add
weight according to

\[
\frac{dw_i}{dt}
=
-\lambda_i w_i
+
\eta_i r_i(t),
\]

where \(r_i(t)\ge0\) is reinforcement signal and
\(\eta_i\ge0\) is learning rate.

For constant reinforcement \(r_i(t)=r_i\), the solution is

\[
w_i(t)
=
w_i(0)e^{-\lambda_i t}
+
\frac{\eta_i r_i}{\lambda_i}
\left(1-e^{-\lambda_i t}\right).
\]

Thus

\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}w_i(t)
=
\frac{\eta_i r_i}{\lambda_i}.
\]

\begin{proposition}{Reinforced Memory Equilibrium}{reinforced-memory-equilibrium}
  Under constant reinforcement, the equilibrium trace
  weight is
  \[
    w_i^*
=
    \frac{\eta_i r_i}{\lambda_i}.
  \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  At equilibrium,
  \[
    \frac{dw_i}{dt}=0.
  \]
  Therefore
  \[
    -\lambda_i w_i+\eta_i r_i=0,
  \]
  and hence
  \[
    w_i^*=\frac{\eta_i r_i}{\lambda_i}.
  \]
\end{proof}

\subsection{Kernel Memory as Projection}

Let

\[
\Phi:X\to\mathcal H_K
\]

be the feature map into the reproducing-kernel Hilbert
space associated with \(K\), so that

\[
K(x,e)
=
\langle \Phi(x),\Phi(e)\rangle_{\mathcal H_K}.
\]

Then

\[
M(x,t)
=
\left\langle
\Phi(x),
\sum_i w_i(t)\Phi(e_i)
\right\rangle_{\mathcal H_K}.
\]

Define the memory vector

\[
m(t)
=
\sum_i w_i(t)\Phi(e_i).
\]

Then

\[
M(x,t)
=
\langle\Phi(x),m(t)\rangle_{\mathcal H_K}.
\]

\begin{proposition}{Kernel Representation of Memory}{kernel-memory-representation}
  The MEM\(|\)8 memory field is a linear functional in
  feature space.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  The result follows by substituting the reproducing
  property
  \[
    K(x,e_i)
=
    \langle\Phi(x),\Phi(e_i)\rangle
  \]
  into the definition of \(M(x,t)\).
\end{proof}

Thus memory recall is nonlinear in original cue space but
linear in kernel feature space.

\subsection{False Recall}

False recall occurs when

\[
M(x,t)>\theta
\]

for a cue \(x\) not corresponding to any intended stored
event.

Let

\[
B_i(r)
=
\{x:d(x,e_i)<r\}
\]

denote the intended recall neighborhood of \(e_i\).

Define the intended region

\[
B(r)
=
\bigcup_i B_i(r).
\]

\begin{definition}{False Recall Region}{false-recall-region}
  The false recall region is
  \[
    F_\theta(t)
=
    E_\theta(t)\setminus B(r).
  \]
\end{definition}

Its measure is

\[
\mu(F_\theta(t)).
\]

\begin{definition}{False Recall Rate}{false-recall-rate}
  The false recall rate is
  \[
    \mathrm{FR}(t)
=
    \frac{\mu(F_\theta(t))}
    {\mu(E_\theta(t))}
  \]
  whenever \(\mu(E_\theta(t))>0\).
\end{definition}

This gives a geometric measure of hallucinated recall,
confabulation, or spurious associative activation.

\subsection{Memory Capacity Bound}

Suppose the cue space has finite measure

\[
\mu(X)<\infty.
\]

Assume each event has a recall neighborhood of radius
\(r_\theta\) and that these neighborhoods must remain
disjoint for unambiguous recall.

If each ball has measure

\[
b_\theta=\mu(B_i(r_\theta)),
\]

then the maximum number of noninterfering memories is
bounded by

\[
N_{\max}
\le
\frac{\mu(X)}{b_\theta}.
\]

\begin{theorem}{Noninterference Capacity Bound}{noninterference-capacity}
  If unambiguous recall requires pairwise disjoint
  ecphoric neighborhoods, then
  \[
    N
    \le
    \frac{\mu(X)}{b_\theta}.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  If recall neighborhoods are disjoint, then
  \[
    \mu\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^N B_i(r_\theta)\right)
=
    \sum_{i=1}^N \mu(B_i(r_\theta))
=
    Nb_\theta.
  \]
  Since the union is contained in \(X\),
  \[
    Nb_\theta\le\mu(X).
  \]
  Therefore
  \[
    N\le\frac{\mu(X)}{b_\theta}.
  \]
\end{proof}

\subsection{Repair Interpretation}

A memory trace is recoverable when an appropriate cue can
reconstruct it. Thus recoverability may be written as

\[
\reco(e_i)
=
\mu
\left(
\{x:w_iK(x,e_i)>\theta\}
\right).
\]

For a Gaussian trace with \(w_i>\theta\),

\[
\reco(e_i)
=
\mu(B_i(r_\theta)).
\]

Hence recoverability is proportional to the measure of the
ecphoric basin.

\begin{proposition}{Recoverability--Basin Relation}{recoverability-basin}
  For single-trace Gaussian memory, recoverability
  increases monotonically with
  \[
    \sigma
    \sqrt{
      2\log\left(\frac{w_i}{\theta}\right)
    }.
  \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  The ecphoric basin radius is
  \[
    r_\theta
=
    \sigma
    \sqrt{
      2\log\left(\frac{w_i}{\theta}\right)
    }.
  \]
  In ordinary metric spaces, ball measure is monotone in
  radius. Therefore recoverability increases monotonically
  with \(r_\theta\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Summary Formulae}

The MEM\(|\)8 architecture may be summarized by

\[
M(x,t)
=
\sum_i w_i(t)K(x,e_i),
\]

\[
E_\theta(t)
=
\{x:M(x,t)>\theta\},
\]

\[
\chi_\theta(x,t)
=
\mathbf 1_{M(x,t)>\theta},
\]

\[
H_{\mathrm{rec}}
=
-\sum_i\alpha_i\log\alpha_i,
\]

\[
\mathrm{FR}(t)
=
\frac{\mu(E_\theta(t)\setminus B(r))}
{\mu(E_\theta(t))},
\]

and

\[
\reco(e_i)
=
\mu
\left(
\{x:w_iK(x,e_i)>\theta\}
\right).
\]

\section{Computational Admissibility}

For computational system $C$, reachable volume
$V_C(t) = |R_C(t)|$.

\begin{theorem}{Computational Admissibility Theorem}{comp-adm-app}
  A computation is generatively admissible iff
  $\frac{d}{dt}V_C(t) \ge 0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}{Computational Conservation Law}{comp-conserve}
  If admissible volume is preserved, distinction capacity
  cannot collapse:
  $\frac{dA}{dt} \ge 0 \implies \frac{dD}{dt} \ge -\epsilon$
  for bounded projection loss $\epsilon$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Admissible volume bounds reachability volume.
  Reachability volume bounds distinction capacity
  (\cref{thm:reach-dist}).
  The result follows by transitivity.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Computational Reachability Entropy]{comp-reachability-entropy}

\[
S_C(t)
=
\log V_C(t).
\]

\end{definition}

\begin{prop}

\[
\frac{dS_C}{dt}
=
\frac{1}{V_C}
\frac{dV_C}{dt}.
\]

\end{prop}

\begin{proof}

Direct differentiation gives

\[
\frac{d}{dt}
\log V_C
=
\frac{1}{V_C}
\frac{dV_C}{dt}.
\]

\end{proof}


% ============================================================
%  APPENDIX C: BIOLOGICAL BACKGROUND
% ============================================================
\chapter{Biological Background}
\label{app:bio}
\section{Life as Distinction Preservation}

Let

\[
\Sigma(t)
\]

denote a biological system and let

\[
D(t)
\]

denote its distinction capacity.

A distinction is any physically realized separation whose
maintenance contributes to continued organization.

Examples include membrane boundaries, genetic coding
relationships, immune recognition classes, neural feature
maps, developmental identities, and ecological niches.

\begin{definition}{Biological Distinction}{biological-distinction}
A biological distinction is an equivalence relation

\[
\sim_B
\]

whose preservation contributes to persistence of the
system.
\end{definition}

The total distinction capacity is

\[
D(t)
=
\log |\mathcal P_B(t)|
\]

where

\[
\mathcal P_B(t)
\]

is the biologically relevant partition at time \(t\).

\begin{definition}{Distinction Decay Rate}{distinction-decay}
The distinction decay rate is

\[
\delta_D
=
-\frac{dD}{dt}.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}{Repair Rate}{repair-rate}
The distinction repair rate is

\[
\rho_D
=
\frac{dD_R}{dt},
\]

where \(D_R\) denotes restored distinction capacity.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Biological Persistence Theorem}{bio-persistence}
If

\[
\rho_D
<
\delta_D,
\]

then biological distinction capacity eventually vanishes.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The net distinction balance is

\[
\frac{dD}{dt}
=
\rho_D-\delta_D.
\]

If

\[
\rho_D-\delta_D<0,
\]

then

\[
D(t)
=
D(0)
+
(\rho_D-\delta_D)t.
\]

Therefore

\[
D(t)\rightarrow0
\]

in finite time whenever distinction capacity is bounded
below by zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
Long-term persistence requires

\[
\rho_D
\ge
\delta_D.
\]
\end{corollary}
\section{Thermodynamic Setting}

Let

\[
\Sigma
\]

denote a biological system embedded in environment

\[
E.
\]

The Second Law implies

\[
\frac{dS_{\Sigma\cup E}}{dt}
\ge0.
\]

The entropy balance for an open system is

\[
\frac{dS_\Sigma}{dt}
=
\sigma
-
J_S,
\]

where

\[
\sigma
\ge0
\]

is internal entropy production and

\[
J_S
\]

is entropy export.

\begin{definition}{Thermodynamic Repair Capacity}{therm-repair-capacity}
The repair capacity is

\[
\kappa_T
=
J_S-\sigma.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Thermodynamic Persistence Criterion}{therm-persistence}
A biological system can maintain local order only if

\[
J_S
>
\sigma.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
From the entropy balance equation,

\[
\frac{dS_\Sigma}{dt}
=
\sigma-J_S.
\]

Local entropy decreases only if

\[
\frac{dS_\Sigma}{dt}<0.
\]

Therefore

\[
J_S>\sigma.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
Repair is necessarily dissipative.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Entropy export requires irreversible processes and
therefore positive environmental entropy production.
\end{proof}
\section{Autopoiesis}

Let

\[
P
=
\{p_1,\ldots,p_n\}
\]

denote the set of productive processes.

Define a directed graph

\[
G=(P,E)
\]

where

\[
(p_i,p_j)\in E
\]

iff process \(p_i\) contributes to production of
\(p_j\).

\begin{definition}{Autopoietic Closure}{autopoietic-closure}
A production network is autopoietically closed if every
vertex lies on a directed cycle.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Autopoietic Closure Theorem}{autopoietic-closure}
Autopoietic systems correspond to strongly connected
directed graphs.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose the graph is strongly connected.

For every pair

\[
p_i,p_j
\]

there exists a directed path from

\[
p_i
\]

to

\[
p_j.
\]

In particular every productive process is reachable from
the others.

Therefore each process participates in production of every
other process through some finite chain.

Conversely, if closure holds for all productive
components, every process must be reachable from every
other.

Hence the graph is strongly connected.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
Removal of a cut vertex may destroy autopoietic closure.
\end{corollary}

\section{Genomes as Historical Archives}

Evolutionary information:
$I_E(G) = \log[P(G \mid \text{selection})/P(G \mid \text{random})]$.
Large values correspond to long histories of selective
retention. Let $\mu$ = mutation rate, $\rho$ = repair rate.

\begin{theorem}{Genomic Stability Theorem}{genomic-stability}
  If $\rho < \mu$, long-term distinction preservation
  is impossible: expected damage grows as $(\mu-\rho)t$.
\end{theorem}

\section{Immune System as Admissibility Filter}

Healthy states $A \subseteq X$; immune classifier
$f: X \to \{0,1\}$.
Type-I error ($f(x)=0$, $x \in A$): missed pathogen.
Type-II error ($f(x)=1$, $x \notin A$): autoimmunity.
Cancer corresponds to systematic Type-I failure.

\begin{theorem}{Cancer Continuation Theorem}{cancer-cont}
  Tumour success and organism admissibility are
  anti-correlated: increasing tumour continuation reduces
  organism admissible volume $V_A(t) \to 0$.
\end{theorem}
\section{Developmental Landscapes}

Let

\[
U(x)
\]

denote Waddington's developmental potential.

Dynamics satisfy

\[
\dot x
=
-\nabla U.
\]

\begin{theorem}{Developmental Stability Theorem}{developmental-stability}
Every strict local minimum of \(U\) is Lyapunov stable.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Consider

\[
V(x)
=
U(x)-U(x^*).
\]

Since \(x^*\) is a strict minimum,

\[
V(x)>0
\]

for

\[
x\neq x^*.
\]

Differentiating along trajectories,

\[
\dot V
=
\nabla U\cdot \dot x.
\]

Substituting

\[
\dot x=-\nabla U
\]

gives

\[
\dot V
=
-\|\nabla U\|^2
\le0.
\]

Hence \(V\) is a Lyapunov function.
\end{proof}

\section{Evolutionary Reachability}

Evolutionary volume $V_E(t) = |R_t|$ where $R_t$ is
the set of genotypes reachable through mutation and
selection.

\begin{theorem}{Evolutionary Constraint Theorem}{evo-constraint}
  Increasing specialisation generally decreases $V_E$
  (by Theorem~\ref{thm:reach-mono}).
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}{Evolutionary Distinction Theorem}{evo-dist}
  Major evolutionary transitions increase total
  distinction capacity: $D_{n+1} > D_n$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  New organisational levels introduce distinctions
  unavailable at lower scales
  \parencite{maynard-smith1995major}.
\end{proof}

\section{Neural Criticality}

Neural branching ratio $\sigma$:
subcritical ($\sigma<1$), critical ($\sigma=1$),
supercritical ($\sigma>1$).

\begin{theorem}{Critical Avalanche Theorem}{critical-avalanche}
For a branching process with branching ratio

\[
\sigma,
\]

expected avalanche size satisfies

\[
\langle s\rangle
=
\frac{1}{1-\sigma}
\]

for

\[
\sigma<1.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The expected descendants are

\[
1+\sigma+\sigma^2+\cdots.
\]

Summing the geometric series yields

\[
\frac{1}{1-\sigma}.
\]
\end{proof}

As

\[
\sigma\rightarrow1^{-},
\]

the expected avalanche size diverges.

\section{Ecological Diversity and Repair}

Ecosystem as distinction ecology:
species $\leftrightarrow$ distinction-maintaining processes;
food web $\leftrightarrow$ repair dependencies.

\begin{theorem}{Ecological Diversity Theorem}{eco-diversity-app}
  Increasing species diversity weakly increases repair
  capacity (Diversity--Repair Theorem,
  \cref{thm:diversity-repair}).
\end{theorem}

\section{Biological Admissibility}

Let

\[
X_B
\]

denote the biological state space of an organism,
population, lineage, or ecosystem.

Not every biologically reachable state is viable.
Many reachable states correspond to extinction,
sterility, developmental failure, mutational meltdown,
ecological collapse, or loss of adaptive capacity.

The central object of biological admissibility theory is
therefore not the reachable set itself but the subset of
reachable states compatible with continued adaptive
possibility.

\begin{definition}{Biological Admissible Region}{bio-adm-region}
The biological admissible region is the set

\[
\mathcal A_B
\subseteq
X_B
\]

consisting of all states from which adaptive,
developmental, ecological, and reproductive processes
remain viable.
\end{definition}

Examples include:

\[
\text{viable genomes},
\]

\[
\text{functional immune states},
\]

\[
\text{stable ecological configurations},
\]

and

\[
\text{developmentally accessible phenotypes}.
\]

The admissible region may itself evolve through time as
environments, developmental constraints, and ecological
interactions change.

\subsection{Biological Admissibility Volume}

Let

\[
\mu
\]

be a measure on biological state space.

\begin{definition}{Biological Admissibility Volume}{bio-adm-volume}
The biological admissibility volume is

\[
V_B(t)
=
\mu(\mathcal A_B(t)).
\]
\end{definition}

This quantity measures the size of the viable future
region available to a biological system.

Large admissibility volume corresponds to abundant future
possibilities.

Small admissibility volume corresponds to fragility.

Vanishing admissibility volume corresponds to biological
collapse.

\begin{definition}{Biological Admissibility Entropy}{bio-adm-entropy}
The logarithmic admissibility volume is

\[
S_B(t)
=
\log V_B(t).
\]
\end{definition}

The quantity

\[
S_B
\]

measures the effective number of biologically viable
futures.

\begin{proposition}{Admissibility Entropy Evolution}{bio-adm-entropy}
For

\[
V_B(t)>0,
\]

\[
\frac{dS_B}{dt}
=
\frac{1}{V_B}
\frac{dV_B}{dt}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since

\[
S_B
=
\log V_B,
\]

differentiation gives

\[
\frac{dS_B}{dt}
=
\frac{1}{V_B}
\frac{dV_B}{dt}.
\]
\end{proof}

\subsection{Generative Biological Systems}

Repair preserves existing biological distinctions.

Regeneration preserves the ability to generate new
distinctions.

This distinction motivates the central criterion of
biological admissibility.

\begin{definition}{Generative Biological System}{gen-bio-system}
A biological system is generative if

\[
\frac{dV_B}{dt}
\ge 0.
\]
\end{definition}

Equivalently,

\[
\frac{dS_B}{dt}
\ge 0.
\]

Such systems preserve or expand the space of viable
future biological configurations.

\begin{theorem}{Biological Generativity Theorem}{bio-generativity}
If

\[
\frac{dV_B}{dt}
\ge 0,
\]

then biological admissibility entropy cannot decrease.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:bio-adm-entropy},

\[
\frac{dS_B}{dt}
=
\frac{1}{V_B}
\frac{dV_B}{dt}.
\]

Since

\[
V_B>0
\]

and

\[
\frac{dV_B}{dt}\ge0,
\]

it follows that

\[
\frac{dS_B}{dt}\ge0.
\]
\end{proof}

\subsection{Fitness Versus Admissibility}

Classical evolutionary theory often focuses upon
short-term reproductive success.

Let

\[
F(x)
\]

denote fitness.

Evolutionary dynamics frequently assume selection
maximizes

\[
F.
\]

However, fitness and admissibility need not coincide.

\begin{ex}
A parasite may increase immediate reproductive success
while reducing long-term ecological viability.

A tumour lineage may increase cellular replication while
destroying host survival.

A highly specialized organism may outperform competitors
within a narrow niche while reducing future adaptive
capacity.
\end{ex}

These examples motivate a distinction between fitness and
admissibility.

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Fitness}{adm-fitness}
The admissibility fitness of state \(x\) is

\[
F_A(x)
=
F(x)V_B(x).
\]
\end{definition}

This quantity weights reproductive success by preservation
of future possibility.

\begin{prop}
A trajectory maximizing fitness need not maximize
admissibility fitness.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Let

\[
x_1,x_2
\]

satisfy

\[
F(x_1)>F(x_2)
\]

but

\[
V_B(x_1)\ll V_B(x_2).
\]

Then

\[
F_A(x_1)
=
F(x_1)V_B(x_1)
\]

may be smaller than

\[
F_A(x_2)
=
F(x_2)V_B(x_2).
\]

Therefore fitness maximization and admissibility
maximization can diverge.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Evolutionary Reachability}

Let

\[
R_E(t)
\]

denote the set of genotypes reachable through mutation,
selection, recombination, and developmental
transformation.

Define evolutionary admissibility volume

\[
V_{BE}(t)
=
\mu
\bigl(
R_E(t)
\cap
\mathcal A_B(t)
\bigr).
\]

\begin{definition}{Evolutionary Admissibility}{evo-adm}
The quantity

\[
V_{BE}
\]

measures the volume of reachable and biologically viable
futures.
\end{definition}

A lineage may possess a large reachable set while having
a small admissible reachable set.

The latter quantity is generally more relevant to
long-term persistence.

\subsection{Evolutionary Curvature}

The geometry of biological possibility may be described
through curvature.

Define the biological admissibility potential

\[
\Psi_B
=
-\log V_B.
\]

\begin{definition}{Biological Admissibility Curvature}{bio-adm-curvature}
The biological admissibility curvature tensor is

\[
K^{(B)}_{ij}
=
\nabla_i\nabla_j\Psi_B.
\]
\end{definition}

The associated scalar curvature is

\[
K_B
=
g^{ij}
K^{(B)}_{ij}.
\]

\begin{prop}
Positive biological admissibility curvature corresponds
to local contraction of viable evolutionary futures.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Positive Hessian curvature implies local convexity of

\[
\Psi_B.
\]

Since

\[
\Psi_B=-\log V_B,
\]

this corresponds to local contraction of admissible
volume.
\end{proof}

\begin{prop}
Negative biological admissibility curvature corresponds
to local expansion of adaptive possibility.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Negative curvature implies local expansion of

\[
V_B.
\]

Hence nearby perturbations increase future viable
possibility.
\end{proof}

\subsection{ Biological Collapse}

\begin{definition}{Biological Collapse}{bio-collapse}
Biological collapse occurs when

\[
V_B(t)
\rightarrow0.
\]
\end{definition}

Examples include:

\[
\text{extinction},
\]

\[
\text{mutational meltdown},
\]

\[
\text{ecological collapse},
\]

\[
\text{irreversible developmental failure},
\]

and

\[
\text{terminal immune dysfunction}.
\]

\begin{theorem}{Biological Collapse Theorem}{bio-collapse-theorem}
If

\[
V_B(t)\rightarrow0,
\]

then

\[
S_B(t)
=
\log V_B(t)
\rightarrow
-\infty.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Immediate from

\[
\lim_{x\to0^+}\log x=-\infty.
\]
\end{proof}

\subsection{ Adaptive Radiation and Admissibility Expansion}

Adaptive radiations provide examples of admissibility
growth.

Suppose a lineage enters a region of ecological space
containing unoccupied niches.

The corresponding admissible region expands from

\[
\mathcal A_B
\]

to

\[
\mathcal A_B'.
\]

If

\[
\mathcal A_B
\subset
\mathcal A_B',
\]

then

\[
V_B'
>
V_B.
\]

\begin{prop}
Adaptive radiations correspond to positive admissibility
growth.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Expansion of viable niche structure increases admissible
volume by monotonicity of measure.
\end{proof}

\subsection{ Biological Admissibility Principle}

The preceding results may be summarized by a single
criterion.

\begin{theorem}{Biological Admissibility Principle}{bio-adm-principle}
A biological trajectory is generatively admissible if and
only if it preserves or expands biological admissibility
volume:

\[
\frac{dV_B}{dt}
\ge0.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Preservation or expansion of biological admissibility is
precisely the definition of generative biological
admissibility.
\end{proof}

This criterion distinguishes mere continuation from
regeneration. A lineage, organism, or ecosystem may
persist while progressively shrinking its future
possibility space. Generatively admissible biological
systems instead preserve or expand the production of
future distinctions, future adaptations, and future
evolutionary trajectories.

% ============================================================
%  APPENDIX D: PROOF DEPENDENCY TABLES
% ============================================================
\chapter{Proof Dependency Tables}
\label{app:deps}

\section{Purpose}

The argument of this book is cumulative.
Every theorem is derived from previous theorems.
This appendix exposes that dependency structure as a
directed acyclic graph $\mathcal{G} = (V, E)$
where vertices are results and directed edges encode
proof dependence.

\section{Dependency Relation}

$T_i \prec T_j$ iff theorem $T_j$ requires $T_i$
in its proof. The dependency graph is
$V = \{T_1, \ldots, T_n\}$ and
$E = \{(T_i, T_j) : T_i \prec T_j\}$.

\begin{theorem}{Acyclicity Theorem}{acyclicity}
  The theorem dependency graph is a directed acyclic
  graph (DAG).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  All theorems are introduced in chapter order.
  A theorem may only depend upon previously established
  results. Cycles are therefore impossible.
\end{proof}

\section{Foundational Basis}

Define the foundational basis:
\[
  \mathcal{B}
  = \{A_1, A_2, A_3, A_4, A_5\},
\]
where $A_1 =$ Axiom of Distinction,
$A_2 =$ Information--Distinction Theorem,
$A_3 =$ Distinction--Entropy Duality,
$A_4 =$ Law of Historical Compression,
$A_5 =$ Law of Recoverability.

\begin{theorem}{Foundation Theorem}{foundation}
  For every theorem $T$ in the text, $\exists A_i \in
  \mathcal{B}$ such that $A_i \prec^* T$
  (transitive closure).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Inspection of the proof graph. All later chapters
  invoke recoverability, history, entropy, information,
  or distinction structure.
\end{proof}

\section{Dependency Depth Table}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lc}
\toprule
Result & Approximate depth \\
\midrule
Axiom of Distinction & 0 \\
Information--Distinction Theorem & 1 \\
Distinction--Entropy Duality & 2 \\
Law of Historical Compression & 3 \\
Law of Recoverability & 4 \\
Repair Existence Theorem & 5 \\
Persistent Anomaly Theorem & 6 \\
Regeneration Theorem & 7 \\
Future Cone Theorem & 8 \\
Admissibility Existence Theorem & 9 \\
Future Distinction Optimality Theorem & 10 \\
Preservation of Possibility Theorem & 11 \\
Ecology of Distinctions Theorem & 12 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\section{Layer-by-Layer Dependency Matrices}

\paragraph{Repair layer.}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
\toprule
& $A_5$ & Repair Exist. & Closure & Entropy \\
\midrule
$A_5$ & 1&1&1&1 \\
Repair Exist. & 0&1&1&1 \\
Closure & 0&0&1&1 \\
Entropy & 0&0&0&1 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Geometry layer.}

& Reach. & Adm. & Future Vol. & GAT \\
\midrule
Reachability & 1&1&1&1 \\
Admissibility & 0&1&1&1 \\
Future Volume & 0&0&1&1 \\
GAT & 0&0&0&1 \\

\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\section{Failure Layer Dependency}

\begin{theorem}{Failure Dependency Theorem}{failure-dep}
  No theorem in Chapter~\ref{ch:geometry-failure} is
  derivable without Chapter~\ref{ch:repair}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Persistent anomalies are defined relative to repair
  operators. All later results inherit this dependence.
\end{proof}

\section{Master Dependency Chain}

The entire book can be represented as:

\[
\begin{array}{c}
\text{Distinction} \\
\downarrow \\
\text{Information} \\
\downarrow \\
\text{Entropy} \\
\downarrow \\
\text{History} \\
\downarrow \\
\text{Recoverability} \\
\downarrow \\
\text{Repair} \\
\downarrow \\
\text{Failure} \\
\downarrow \\
\text{Intelligence} \\
\downarrow \\
\text{Regeneration} \\
\downarrow \\
\text{Ecology} \\
\downarrow \\
\text{Reachability} \\
\downarrow \\
\text{Admissibility} \\
\downarrow \\
\text{GAT}
\end{array}
\]

\section{Realization Functors}

Each realization chapter corresponds to a functor
$F_i : \mathcal{T} \to \mathcal{D}_i$ mapping the
abstract theorem category $\mathcal{T}$ into a domain:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
Functor & Domain \\
\midrule
$F_\mathrm{RSVP}$ & Physical fields \\
$F_\mathrm{Gravity}$ & Distinction dynamics \\
$F_\mathrm{Cosmo}$ & Expyrotic renewal \\
$F_\mathrm{Semantic}$ & Meaning manifolds \\
$F_\mathrm{Conscious}$ & Self-reconstruction \\
$F_\mathrm{Pref}$ & Admissibility gradients \\
$F_\mathrm{Flow}$ & Distinction transport \\
$F_\mathrm{Spherepop}$ & History-native computation \\
$F_\mathrm{HYDRA}$ & Multi-module repair \\
$F_\mathrm{TARTAN}$ & Multiscale tiling \\
$F_\mathrm{Fiscal}$ & Policy reachability \\
$F_\mathrm{Gov}$ & Institutional repair \\
$F_\mathrm{Science}$ & Epistemic regeneration \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{theorem}{Closure Theorem}{dep-closure}
  The transitive closure of GAT contains every major
  theorem in the text: $\mathcal{B} \subseteq \mathcal{G}_A$
  where $\mathcal{G}_A = \{T : T \prec^* \text{GAT}\}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  GAT $\prec^*$ Admissibility $\prec^*$ Reachability
  $\prec^*$ Regeneration $\prec^*$ Repair
  $\prec^*$ Recoverability $\prec^*$ History
  $\prec^*$ Entropy $\prec^*$ Information
  $\prec^*$ Distinction ($= A_1$).
  Hence $\mathcal{B} \subseteq \mathcal{G}_A$.
\end{proof}

\section{Minimal Axiom Counts}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lc}
\toprule
Theorem & $N(T)$ (foundational axioms required) \\
\midrule
Information--Distinction & 1 \\
Entropy & 2 \\
History Primacy & 3 \\
Repair Existence & 5 \\
Regeneration & 6 \\
Future Cone & 7 \\
Admissibility Existence & 8 \\
Future Distinction Optimality & 8 \\
Preservation of Possibility & 8 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The entire monograph may be viewed as a proof that the
Generative Admissibility Principle is a consequence of
the Axiom of Distinction together with the mathematics
of entropy, history, repair, regeneration, and
reachability.
